title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "knowledge/algorithms/heterogeneous-execution.md", "sha256": "8fd777e06b150eaba4e3288ccb188adbb975e37c28c77a72a9b6878a70e027f4", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:3863]\n# K-ALG-020：CPU–GPU 计算放置：传数据、传结果与共同提交\n\n异构执行可以按层、算子、输出分片或专家分配计算。决策比较的是完整依赖路径上的计算、传输、同步和容量，而不是孤立的 CPU 与 GPU 峰值算力。\n\n## 三类候选先保持同一函数\n\n第一类让 CPU 保存部分权重、需要时搬到 GPU 计算；第二类让这些权重对应的计算留在 CPU，只交换输入/输出；第三类让 CPU/GPU 同时处理同一层不同部分，再合并结果。若全权重能合法常驻 GPU，也应保留为对照，不能人为引入搬运再证明异构更快。\n\n按输出通道切分 Y=XW 时，双方的结果可以按原通道位置拼接；按归约轴切分则需要求部分和。norm、softmax 或非线性跨越分片时，不一定允许先局部应用再合并。两边的数据布局、dtype、尺度和累计顺序必须服从相同数值契约。\n\n## 成本比较和共享资源\n\n冷权重大小 W𝒸、输入输出交换量 A、链路带宽 Bₗ、CPU 冷部分时间 T𝒸、GPU 冷部分时间 T𝗀。简单的搬权重路径约为 W𝒸/Bₗ+T𝗀；留在 CPU 路径约为 A/Bₗ+T𝒸+同步/合并。可以重叠的区间再按依赖图处理，不能把原本串行的传输全部视为隐藏。\n\nCPU 计算还会与 tokenization、I/O 和 DMA 竞争核与主存带宽；GPU 冷/热部分也可能竞争同一矩阵或内存资源。把它们放到不同线程或 stream 不会自动产生独立能力。容量上同时计权重副本、输入/结果缓冲与在途代次。\n\n## Attention 留在 CPU 的一种条件\n\n若长历史 KV 已在主存，搬整个历史到 GPU 的字节可能远大于交换 query 和 attention 输出。设相同形状下历史为 N 个位置，KV 流量随 N 增长，而单步 query/结果不随历史线性增长；[FlexGen](../sources/S-FLEXGEN.md) §4.2 据此讨论把部分 decode attention 交给 CPU。\n\n这不是所有长历史都适合 CPU 的结论。CPU 的 KV 读取、点积、softmax、布局和线程开销仍需计算；多个请求会竞争主存。若模型采用窗口、MLA、压缩或状态已经在 HBM，原字节比例又会改变。把计算移过去必须实现同一 mask、位置、归一化与精度关系。\n\n## 热点神经元路线的前提\n\n[PowerInfer v1](../sources/S-POWERINFER.md) 通过离线激活统计安排热点在 GPU、冷部分在 CPU，再用预测器和稀疏算子减少执行工作。其评估包括 OPT 以及特定 ReLU/ReGLU 模型变体；不能据此假设普通稠密模型都具有相同稀疏性。\n\n预测为活跃但实际为零可能增加工作，预测为不活跃而实际有贡献则可能改变输出。若要保持原函数，必须有补算/验证或其他足够条件；若允许近似，则把预测器、校准分布和质量回归纳入模型契约。热点统计只表示给定分布下的访问机会，输入变化或 batch 增大都可能改变冷部分的规模。\n\n## 场景：同一冷权重的三种放置\n\n以下全部时间与带宽为设定值。某层冷权重 512 MiB，设备容量不足以永久容纳它，链路为 16 GiB/s；CPU 冷部分计算设为 8 ms，GPU 冷部分设为 1 ms，GPU 热部分设为 6 ms。CPU 路径全部输入与结果交换合计 2 MiB，额外同步 1 ms，结果合并 0.5 ms。\n\n搬权重需 31.25 ms，即使与热部分充分重叠，仍需再执行冷部分；含合并的理想路径约为 max(31.25,6)+1+0.5=32.75 ms。CPU/GPU 分工若在共同输入就绪后可独立执行，则约为 max(6,8+0.1221+1)+0.5=9.6221 ms。这个计算只证明在这些假设下存在比较优势，真实共享带宽、依赖与计算时间还需测量。\n\n第三个候选是把整层留在 CPU，减少层内同步，却让热部分也失去 GPU 计算机会；其总时间必须独立测量。若增加 batch 后，CPU 冷部分变成 80 ms，而搬运加 GPU 计算只需约 40 ms，选择可能反转。若容量允许全部驻留 GPU，之前 31.25 ms 的搬运项消失，也必须重新比较。\n\n### 从执行依赖到验证\n\n为一次层执行记录输入版本、双方分片、各自完成事件、结果原位置和输出代次。两边成功后才提交共同输出；一边失败不能把另一边的局部结果当成完整层。取消后等待既有访问结束，再回收缓冲；迟到 CPU 结果不能被接到新一轮 GPU 输出上。\n\n语义检查先用固定稠密参考验证分片/拼接或部分和，再检查不同完成顺序、空分片、非整除边界和重复使用缓冲。若加入稀疏预测，另做精确稀疏参考、误预测与任务质量测试，不能用固定 seed 文本偶然相同替代这些关系。\n\n性能记录每段 CPU/GPU 时间、实际交换字节、重叠区间、主存竞争与用户时延。扫描 batch、冷比例、历史长度和其他主机负载；逐层局部收益还需经过完整模型累加与服务调度验证。交互小批、长 KV 在主存或明显热点是可研究条件，不能直接构成通用的异构执行规则。\n\n## 具体系统中的组合限制\n\n[FlexGen v1](../sources/S-FLEXGEN.md) §5 为减少迁移字节，将权重和 KV 存为低位表示并在计算前恢复 FP16；由于当时 CPU 解压开销明显，其实现启用量化时关闭 CPU attention delegation。因此，CPU attention 减少搬运与低位缓存减少容量是可分别研究的机制，不能直接把同一论文两种配置的收益相乘。若在新的 CPU 内核中组合它们，需重新测解码、主存读取、attention 与主机其他工作，并独立验证量化质量和状态语义。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance "knowledge/system-semantics/tenant-isolation.md", "sha256": "0ca7501a80e750152a5fb3b9815c77f9e3f9aa393ff7546b3f05375958f88606", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:3863]\n# K-SYS-016：租户隔离：资源份额、缓存命名空间与故障边界\n\n一个推理租户可以同时消耗 CPU、设备计算、KV 容量、adapter 驻留、网络、I/O 与响应缓冲。隔离设计应分别说明谁能够访问哪些对象、谁最多占用多少资源、谁在竞争时优先推进，以及一个故障影响哪些请求。限制其中一项不会自动建立其他保证。\n\n## 从入口身份传到状态拥有者\n\n入口确定的租户、模型/adapter 版本和请求身份，需要传递到调度、缓存、传输与输出。不同租户即使提交相同文本，也不默认取得对方的 KV、检索内容、adapter 或 trace。共享不可变公共前缀应以明确的公共对象契约和版本身份为前提，而不是通过发现相同 hash 就绕过授权。\n\n请求 ID 本身不是访问凭证。对共享缓存，授权范围与数值兼容性同时满足才允许命中；页句柄、远端地址和日志引用也不能只依赖可猜的槽号。过期版本退出目录后，在途访问仍要结束，最后回收时清除对新拥有者不应暴露的内容或重新初始化全部可读范围。\n\ntrace 保存排队、形状、长度、代次与资源事件通常已足以做归因。确需记录原始输入或输出时，把存取范围、保留时间和删除责任纳入同一对象生命周期。租户隔离不是要求所有状态复制一份，而是要求共享的条件和可见范围可验证。\n\n## 主机限额的实际含义\n\n[Linux 6.12 cgroup v2](../sources/S-LINUX-RESOURCES.md) 区分权重、上限、保护与独占分配。CPU 权重决定竞争时的相对份额，CPU 带宽上限约束一段时间可消耗的执行时间，cpuset 约束可运行的 CPU/内存节点；它们不能互相替代。CPU 配额不保证独占 cache、内存带宽或确定的尾延迟。\n\nmemory.high 会引入回收压力和节流，memory.max 在无法回收时可触发 cgroup 内 OOM；文件页缓存、匿名内存与部分内核/网络缓冲也可能计入预算。只统计模型 tensor 会遗漏这些消耗。内存控制器不是所有 GPU 显存对象的自动配额器，GPU 容量仍需执行器和实际设备机制约束。RDMA 对象数、线程/进程数量与存储流量也可能先于字节预算耗尽。\n\n## GPU 共享必须绑定具体机制\n\n下列界限取自固定的 [NVIDIA device plugin v0.16.1](../sources/S-NVIDIA-PLUGIN.md) README。这里说明该版本公开的资源与部署契约，不把插件文档扩展成所有设备、驱动或攻击场景的安全证明。\n\n| 机制 | 该来源明确支持的理解 | 不能由资源名称推出的保证 |\n|---|---|---|\n| 整卡分配 | 将指定设备暴露给容器，区分 UUID/index 等身份传递方式 | 其他宿主进程是否仍可访问、整机故障是否隔离 |\n| CUDA time-slicing | 多个访问份额指向同一底层 GPU；文档明确没有额外的显存与故障域隔离 | 每份额对应固定显存或固定吞吐；申请更多份额按比例得到算力 |\n| 插件的 MPS 共享 | 控制进程按份额限制客户端可用显存与计算能力；该版本只支持完整 GPU，并不支持启用 MIG 的设备 | 与独占物理设备相同的尾延迟、性能无干扰或覆盖所有错误的隔离 |\n| MIG 资源暴露 | 在硬件支持前提下，none/single/mixed 策略决定暴露方式；mixed 可暴露按 profile 命名的资源 | 任意 profile 组合均合法、profile 吞吐线性缩放，或共享宿主/网络/供电故障自动消失 |\n\nMIG、MPS 与 time-slicing 不应被合并成一个“虚拟 GPU”容量数字。MIG profile 需要对应实际硬件支持与配置，多个访问份额也可能继续共享同一 MIG 资源。更细的硬件隔离承诺需要相应固定硬件文档和故障测试；本章不从插件的资源暴露推断未记录的保证。\n\n## 容量、公平与故障预算\n\n设定共享设备的 KV 池为 8 GiB，租户 A/B 各保留 3 GiB，另有 2 GiB 可借用池。借用可在空闲时增加利用率，但 A 已有活跃请求占用的借用状态，不能在 B 到来时立即按账面扣回；必须有合法结束、迁移、重算或拒绝路线。仅设“每租户 10 请求”的限额，会让长上下文租户占据远多于短请求租户的容量。\n\n再设一张卡被 time-slicing 暴露为 8 份。接收 8 个各需要 10 GiB 的服务进程，不会把物理显存变成 80 GiB。共享份额数量只允许更多访问者，模型加载和峰值容量仍需真实字节账。同样，空闲时一个租户借到更多设备时间，不代表竞争时能继续获得相同服务率。\n\n## 验证与选择改变\n\n构造两租户相同输入但不同权限/adapter 的请求，检查缓存、结果、日志与迟到消息无越界归属；再让一个租户持续增长 KV、生成超长响应、启动大量冷 adapter、消耗 CPU 配额与存储流量。观察另一个租户的准入、尾延迟和可完成工作量，不能只检查进程没有退出。\n\n故障测试按已承诺的边界设计：终止一个 worker、制造配额内存不足、使一个共享设备不可用或让网络中断，确认影响范围、可重试语义和清理责任。对全卡故障、宿主故障与租户进程错误分别记录，不能把某一种测试通过推广到全部错误。\n\n严格隔离需求可能要求独占设备或独立执行域；低占用且允许干扰的服务可采用共享。统一基座内的多租户执行节省内存，却把应用调度器变成主要容量和身份边界，必须同时有明确的资源账与独立污染测试。\n\n<!-- title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "references/S-ASGI/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "ba273c91f3e7b8c32f863e8b57145798c3b5c9275073f758a41e1cb0a6f5bee3", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "source_original", "content": "\n===== specs/www.rst =====\n====================================\nHTTP & WebSocket ASGI Message Format\n====================================\n\n**Version**: 2.5 (2024-06-05)\n\nThe HTTP+WebSocket ASGI sub-specification outlines how to transport HTTP/1.1,\nHTTP/2 and WebSocket connections within ASGI.\n\nIt is deliberately intended and designed to be a superset of the WSGI format\nand specifies how to translate between the two for the set of requests that\nare able to be handled by WSGI.\n\n\nSpec Versions\n-------------\n\nThis spec has had the following versions:\n\n* ``2.0``: The first version of the spec, released with ASGI 2.0\n* ``2.1``: Added the ``headers`` key to the WebSocket Accept response\n* ``2.2``: Allow ``None`` in the second item of ``server`` scope value.\n* ``2.3``: Added the ``reason`` key to the WebSocket close event.\n* ``2.4``: Calling ``send()`` on a closed connection should raise an error\n* ``2.5``: Added the ``reason`` key to the WebSocket disconnect event.\n\nSpec versions let you understand what the server you are using understands. If\na server tells you it only supports version ``2.0`` of this spec, then\nsending ``headers`` with a WebSocket Accept message is an error, for example.\n\nThey are separate from the HTTP version or the ASGI version.\n\n\nHTTP\n----\n\nThe HTTP format covers HTTP/1.0, HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2, as the changes in\nHTTP/2 are largely on the transport level. A protocol server should give\ndifferent scopes to different requests on the same HTTP/2 connection, and\ncorrectly multiplex the responses back to the same stream in which they came.\nThe HTTP version is available as a string in the scope.\n\nMultiple header fields with the same name are complex in HTTP. RFC 7230\nstates that for any header field that can appear multiple times, it is exactly\nequivalent to sending that header field only once with all the values joined by\ncommas.\n\nHowever, for HTTP cookies (``Cookie`` and ``Set-Cookie``) the allowed behaviour\ndoes not follow the above rule, and also varies slightly based on the HTTP\nprotocol version:\n\n* For the ``Set-Cookie`` header in HTTP/1.0, HTTP/1.1 and HTTP2.0, it may appear\n  repeatedly, but cannot be concatenated by commas (or anything else) into a\n  single header field.\n\n* For the ``Cookie`` header, in HTTP/1.0 and HTTP/1.1, RFC 7230 and RFC 6265\n  make it clear that the ``Cookie`` header must only be sent once by a\n  user-agent, and must be concatenated into a single octet string using the\n  two-octet delimiter of 0x3b, 0x20 (the ASCII string \"; \"). However in HTTP/2,\n  RFC 9113 states that ``Cookie`` headers MAY appear repeatedly, OR be\n  concatenated using the two-octet delimiter of 0x3b, 0x20\n  (the ASCII string \"; \").\n\nThe ASGI design decision is to transport both request and response headers as\nlists of 2-element ``[name, value]`` lists and preserve headers exactly as they\nwere provided.\n\nFor ASGI applications that support HTTP/2, care should be taken to handle the\nspecial case for ``Cookie`` noted above.\n\nThe HTTP protocol should be signified to ASGI applications with a ``type``\nvalue of ``http``.\n\n\nHTTP Connection Scope\n'''''''''''''''''''''\n\nHTTP connections have a single-request *connection scope* - that is, your\napplication will be called at the start of the request, and will last until\nthe end of that specific request, even if the underlying socket is still open\nand serving multiple requests.\n\nIf you hold a response open for long-polling or similar, the *connection scope*\nwill persist until the response closes from either the client or server side.\n\nThe *connection scope* information passed in ``scope`` contains:\n\n* ``type`` (*Unicode string*) -- ``\"http\"``.\n\n* ``asgi[\"version\"]`` (*Unicode string*) -- Version of the ASGI spec.\n\n* ``asgi[\"spec_version\"]`` (*Unicode string*) -- Version of the ASGI\n  HTTP spec this server understands; for example: ``\"2.0\"``, ``\"2.1\"``, ``\"2.2\"``,\n  etc. Optional; if missing assume ``\"2.0\"``.\n\n* ``http_version`` (*Unicode string*) -- One of ``\"1.0\"``, ``\"1.1\"`` or ``\"2\"``.\n\n* ``method`` (*Unicode string*) -- The HTTP method name, uppercased.\n\n* ``scheme`` (*Unicode string*) -- URL scheme portion (likely ``\"http\"`` or\n  ``\"https\"``). Optional (but must not be empty); default is ``\"http\"``.\n\n* ``path`` (*Unicode string*) -- HTTP request target excluding any query\n  string, with percent-encoded sequences and UTF-8 byte sequences\n  decoded into characters.\n\n* ``raw_path`` (*byte string*) -- The original HTTP path component,\n  excluding any query string, unmodified from the bytes that were\n  received by the web server. Some web server implementations may\n  be unable to provide this. Optional; if missing defaults to ``None``.\n\n* ``query_string`` (*byte string*) -- URL portion after the ``?``,\n  percent-encoded.\n\n* ``root_path`` (*Unicode string*) -- The root path this application\n  is mounted at; same as ``SCRIPT_NAME`` in WSGI. Optional; if missing\n  defaults to ``\"\"``.\n\n* ``headers`` (*Iterable[[byte string, byte string]]*) -- An iterable of\n  ``[name, value]`` two-item iterables, where ``name`` is the header name, and\n  ``value`` is the header value. Order of header values must be preserved from\n  the original HTTP request; order of header names is not important. Duplicates\n  are possible and must be preserved in the message as received. Header names\n  should be lowercased, but it is not required; servers should preserve header case\n  on a best-effort basis. Pseudo headers (present in HTTP/2 and HTTP/3) must be\n  removed; if ``:authority`` is present its value must be added to the start of\n  the iterable with ``host`` as the header name or replace any existing host\n  header already present.\n\n* ``client`` (*Iterable[Unicode string, int]*) -- A two-item iterable\n  of ``[host, port]``, where ``host`` is the remote host's IPv4 or\n  IPv6 address, and ``port`` is the remote port as an\n  integer. Optional; if missing defaults to ``None``.\n\n* ``server`` (*Iterable[Unicode string, Optional[int]]*) -- Either a\n  two-item iterable of ``[host, port]``, where ``host`` is the\n  listening address for this server, and ``port`` is the integer\n  listening port, or ``[path, None]`` where ``path`` is that of the\n  unix socket. Optional; if missing defaults to ``None``.\n\n* ``state`` Optional(*dict[Unicode string, Any]*) -- A copy of the\n  namespace passed into the lifespan corresponding to this request. (See :doc:`lifespan`).\n  Optional; if missing the server does not support this feature.\n\nServers are responsible for handling inbound and outbound chunked transfer\nencodings. A request with a ``chunked`` encoded body should be automatically\nde-chunked by the server and presented to the application as plain body bytes;\na response that is given to the server with no ``Content-Length`` may be chunked\nas the server sees fit.\n\n\nRequest - ``receive`` event\n'''''''''''''''''''''''''''\n\nSent to the application to indicate an incoming request. Most of the request\ninformation is in the connection ``scope``; the body message serves as a way to\nstream large incoming HTTP bodies in chunks, and as a trigger to actually run\nrequest code (as you should not trigger on a connection opening alone).\n\nNote that if the request is being sent using ``Transfer-Encoding: chunked``,\nthe server is responsible for handling this encoding. The ``http.request``\nmessages should contain just the decoded contents of each chunk.\n\nKeys:\n\n* ``type`` (*Unicode string*) -- ``\"http.request\"``.\n\n* ``body`` (*byte string*) -- Body of the request. Optional; if\n  missing defaults to ``b\"\"``. If ``more_body`` is set, treat as start\n  of body and concatenate on further chunks.\n\n* ``more_body`` (*bool*) -- Signifies if there is additional content\n  to come (as part of a Request message). If ``True``, the consuming\n  application should wait until it gets a chunk with this set to\n  ``False``. If ``False``, the request is complete and should be\n  processed. Optional; if missing defaults to ``False``.\n\n\nResponse Start - ``send`` event\n'''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''\n\nSent by the application to start sending a response to the client. Needs to be\nfollowed by at least one response content message.\n\nProtocol servers *need not* flush the data generated by this event to the\nsend buffer until the first *Response Body* event is processed.\nThis may give them more leeway to replace the response with an error response\nin case internal errors occur while handling the request.\n\nYou may send a ``Transfer-Encoding`` header in this message, but the server\nmust ignore it. Servers handle ``Transfer-Encoding`` themselves, and may opt\nto use ``Transfer-Encoding: chunked`` if the application presents a response\nthat has no ``Content-Length`` set.\n\nNote that this is not the same as ``Content-Encoding``, which the application\nstill controls, and which is the appropriate place to set ``gzip`` or other\ncompression flags.\n\nKeys:\n\n* ``type`` (*Unicode string*) -- ``\"http.response.start\"``.\n\n* ``status`` (*int*) -- HTTP status code.\n\n* ``headers`` (*Iterable[[byte string, byte string]]*) -- An iterable\n  of ``[name, value]`` two-item iterables, where ``name`` is the\n  header name, and ``value`` is the header value. Order must be\n  preserved in the HTTP response.  Header names must be\n  lowercased. Optional; if missing defaults to an empty list. Pseudo\n  headers (present in HTTP/2 and HTTP/3) must not be present.\n\n* ``trailers`` (*bool*) -- Signifies if the application will send\n  trailers. If ``True``, the server must wait until it receives a\n  ``\"http.response.trailers\"`` message after the *Response Body* event.\n  Optional; if missing defaults to ``False``.\n\n\nResponse Body - ``send`` event\n''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''\n\nContinues sending a response to the client. Protocol servers must\nflush any data passed to them into the send buffer before returning from a\nsend call. If ``more_body`` is set to ``False``, and the server is not\nexpecting *Response Trailers* this will complete the response.\n\nKeys:\n\n* ``type`` (*Unicode string*) -- ``\"http.response.body\"``.\n\n* ``body`` (*byte string*) -- HTTP body content. Concatenated onto any\n  previous ``body`` values sent in this connection scope. Optional; if\n  missing defaults to ``b\"\"``.\n\n* ``more_body`` (*bool*) -- Signifies if there is additional content\n  to come (as part of a *Response Body* message). If ``False``, and the\n  server is not expecting *Response Trailers* response will be taken as\n  complete and closed, and any further messages on the channel will be\n  ignored. Optional; if missing defaults to ``False``.\n\n\nDisconnected Client - ``send`` exception\n''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''\n\nIf ``send()`` is called on a closed connection the server should raise\na server-specific subclass of ``OSError``. This is not guaranteed, however,\nespecially on older ASGI server implementations (it was introduced in spec\nversion 2.4).\n\nApplications may catch this exception and do cleanup work before\nre-raising it or returning with no exception.\n\nServers must be prepared to catch this exception if they raised it and\nshould not log it as an error in their server logs.\n\n\nDisconnect - ``receive`` event\n''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''\n\nSent to the application if receive is called after a response has been\nsent or after the HTTP connection has been closed. This is mainly useful\nfor long-polling, where you may want to trigger cleanup code if the\nconnection closes early.\n\nOnce you have received this event, you should expect future calls to ``send()``\nto raise an exception, as described above. However, if you have highly\nconcurrent code, you may find calls to ``send()`` erroring slightly before you\nreceive this event.\n\nKeys:\n\n* ``type`` (*Unicode string*) -- ``\"http.disconnect\"``.\n\n\nWebSocket\n---------\n\nWebSockets share some HTTP details - they have a path and headers - but also\nhave more state. Again, most of that state is in the ``scope``, which will live\nas long as the socket does.\n\nWebSocket protocol servers should handle PING/PONG messages themselves, and\nsend PING messages as necessary to ensure the connection is alive.\n\nWebSocket protocol servers should handle message fragmentation themselves,\nand deliver complete messages to the application.\n\nThe WebSocket protocol should be signified to ASGI applications with\na ``type`` value of ``websocket``.\n\n\nWebsocket Connection Scope\n''''''''''''''''''''''''''\n\nWebSocket connections' scope lives as long as the socket itself - if the\napplication dies the socket should be closed, and vice-versa.\n\nThe *connection scope* information passed in ``scope`` contains initial connection\nmetadata (mostly from the HTTP request line and headers):\n\n* ``type`` (*Unicode string*) -- ``\"websocket\"``.\n\n* ``asgi[\"version\"]`` (*Unicode string*) -- The version of the ASGI spec.\n\n* ``asgi[\"spec_version\"]`` (*Unicode string*) -- Version of the ASGI\n  HTTP spec this server understands; one of ``\"2.0\"``, ``\"2.1\"``, ``\"2.2\"`` or\n  ``\"2.3\"``. Optional; if missing assume ``\"2.0\"``.\n\n* ``http_version`` (*Unicode string*) -- One of ``\"1.1\"`` or\n  ``\"2\"``. Optional; if missing default is ``\"1.1\"``.\n\n* ``scheme`` (*Unicode string*) -- URL scheme portion (likely ``\"ws\"`` or\n  ``\"wss\"``). Optional (but must not be empty); default is ``\"ws\"``.\n\n* ``path`` (*Unicode string*) -- HTTP request target excluding any query\n  string, with percent-encoded sequences and UTF-8 byte sequences\n  decoded into characters.\n\n* ``raw_path`` (*byte string*) -- The original HTTP path component,\n  excluding any query string, unmodified from the bytes that were\n  received by the web server. Some web server implementations may\n  be unable to provide this. Optional; if missing defaults to ``None``.\n\n* ``query_string`` (*byte string*) -- URL portion after the\n  ``?``. Optional; if missing or ``None`` default is empty string.\n\n* ``root_path`` (*Unicode string*) -- The root path this application is\n  mounted at; same as ``SCRIPT_NAME`` in WSGI. Optional; if missing\n  defaults to empty string.\n\n* ``headers`` (*Iterable[[byte string, byte string]]*) -- An iterable of\n  ``[name, value]`` two-item iterables, where ``name`` is the header name and\n  ``value`` is the header value. Order should be preserved from the original\n  HTTP request; duplicates are possible and must be preserved in the message\n  as received. Header names should be lowercased, but it is not required;\n  servers should preserve header case on a best-effort basis.\n  Pseudo headers (present in HTTP/2 and HTTP/3) must be removed;\n  if ``:authority`` is present its value must be added to the\n  start of the iterable with ``host`` as the header name\n  or replace any existing host header already present.\n\n* ``client`` (*Iterable[Unicode string, int]*) -- A two-item iterable\n  of ``[host, port]``, where ``host`` is the remote host's IPv4 or\n  IPv6 address, and ``port`` is the remote port. Optional; if missing\n  defaults to ``None``.\n\n* ``server`` (*Iterable[Unicode string, Optional[int]]*) -- Either a\n  two-item iterable of ``[host, port]``, where ``host`` is the\n  listening address for this server, and ``port`` is the integer\n  listening port, or ``[path, None]`` where ``path`` is that of the\n  unix socket. Optional; if missing defaults to ``None``.\n\n* ``subprotocols`` (*Iterable[Unicode string]*) -- Subprotocols the\n  client advertised. Optional; if missing defaults to empty list.\n\n* ``state`` Optional(*dict[Unicode string, Any]*) -- A copy of the\n  namespace passed into the lifespan corresponding to this request. (See :doc:`lifespan`).\n  Optional; if missing the server does not support this feature.\n\n\nConnect - ``receive`` event\n'''''''''''''''''''''''''''\n\nSent to the application when the client initially opens a connection and is about\nto finish the WebSocket handshake.\n\nThis message must be responded to with either an *Accept* message\nor a *Close* message before the socket will pass ``websocket.receive``\nmessages. The protocol server must send this message\nduring the handshake phase of the WebSocket and not complete the handshake\nuntil it gets a reply, returning HTTP status code ``403`` if the connection is\ndenied.\n\nKeys:\n\n* ``type`` (*Unicode string*) -- ``\"websocket.connect\"``.\n\n\nAccept - ``send`` event\n'''''''''''''''''''''''\n\nSent by the application when it wishes to accept an incoming connection.\n\n* ``type`` (*Unicode string*) -- ``\"websocket.accept\"``.\n\n* ``subprotocol`` (*Unicode string*) -- The subprotocol the server\n  wishes to accept. Optional; if missing defaults to ``None``.\n\n* ``headers`` (*Iterable[[byte string, byte string]]*) -- An iterable\n  of ``[name, value]`` two-item iterables, where ``name`` is the\n  header name, and ``value`` is the header value. Order must be\n  preserved in the HTTP response.  Header names must be\n  lowercased. Must not include a header named\n  ``sec-websocket-protocol``; use the ``subprotocol`` key\n  instead. Optional; if missing defaults to an empty list. *Added in\n  spec version 2.1*. Pseudo headers (present in HTTP/2 and HTTP/3)\n  must not be present.\n\n\nReceive - ``receive`` event\n'''''''''''''''''''''''''''\n\nSent to the application when a data message is received from the client.\n\nKeys:\n\n* ``type`` (*Unicode string*) -- ``\"websocket.receive\"``.\n\n* ``bytes`` (*byte string*) -- The message content, if it was binary\n  mode, or ``None``. Optional; if missing, it is equivalent to\n  ``None``.\n\n* ``text`` (*Unicode string*) -- The message content, if it was text\n  mode, or ``None``. Optional; if missing, it is equivalent to\n  ``None``.\n\nExactly one of ``bytes`` or ``text`` must be non-``None``. One or both\nkeys may be present, however.\n\n\nSend - ``send`` event\n'''''''''''''''''''''\n\nSent by the application to send a data message to the client.\n\nKeys:\n\n* ``type`` (*Unicode string*) -- ``\"websocket.send\"``.\n\n* ``bytes`` (*byte string*) -- Binary message content, or ``None``.\n   Optional; if missing, it is equivalent to ``None``.\n\n* ``text`` (*Unicode string*) -- Text message content, or ``None``.\n   Optional; if missing, it is equivalent to ``None``.\n\nExactly one of ``bytes`` or ``text`` must be non-``None``. One or both\nkeys may be present, however.\n\n\n.. _disconnect-receive-event-ws:\n\nDisconnect - ``receive`` event\n''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''\n\nSent to the application when either connection to the client is lost, either from\nthe client closing the connection, the server closing the connection, or loss of the\nsocket.\n\nOnce you have received this event, you should expect future calls to ``send()``\nto raise an exception, as described below. However, if you have highly\nconcurrent code, you may find calls to ``send()`` erroring slightly before you\nreceive this event.\n\nKeys:\n\n* ``type`` (*Unicode string*) -- ``\"websocket.disconnect\"``\n\n* ``code`` (*int*) -- The WebSocket close code, as per the WebSocket spec. If no code\n  was received in the frame from the client, the server should set this to ``1005``\n  (the default value in the WebSocket specification).\n\n* ``reason`` (*Unicode string*) -- A reason given for the disconnect, can\n  be any string. Optional; if missing or ``None`` default is empty\n  string.\n\n\nDisconnected Client - ``send`` exception\n''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''''\n\nIf ``send()`` is called on a closed connection the server should raise\na server-specific subclass of ``OSError``. This is not guaranteed, however,\nespecially on older ASGI server implementations (it was introduced in spec\nversion 2.4).\n\nApplications may catch this exception and do cleanup work before\nre-raising it or returning with no exception.\n\nServers must be prepared to catch this exception if they raised it and\nshould not log it as an error in their server logs.\n\n\nClose - ``send`` event\n''''''''''''''''''''''\n\nSent by the application to tell the server to close the connection.\n\nIf this is sent before the socket is accepted, the server\nmust close the connection with a HTTP 403 error code\n(Forbidden), and not complete the WebSocket handshake; this may present on some\nbrowsers as a different WebSocket error code (such as 1006, Abnormal Closure).\n\nIf this is sent after the socket is accepted, the server must close the socket\nwith the close code passed in the message (or 1000 if none is specified).\n\n* ``type`` (*Unicode string*) -- ``\"websocket.close\"``.\n\n* ``code`` (*int*) -- The WebSocket close code, as per the WebSocket\n  spec.  Optional; if missing defaults to ``1000``.\n\n* ``reason`` (*Unicode string*) -- A reason given for the closure, can\n  be any string. Optional; if missing or ``None`` default is empty\n  string.\n\n\nWSGI Compatibility\n------------------\n\nPart of the design of the HTTP portion of this spec is to make sure it\naligns well with the WSGI specification, to ensure easy adaptability\nbetween both specifications and the ability to keep using WSGI\napplications with ASGI servers.\n\nWSGI applications, being synchronous, must be run in a threadpool in order\nto be served, but otherwise their runtime maps onto the HTTP connection scope's\nlifetime.\n\nThere is an almost direct mapping for the various special keys in\nWSGI's ``environ`` variable to the ``http`` scope:\n\n* ``REQUEST_METHOD`` is the ``method``\n* ``SCRIPT_NAME`` is ``root_path``\n* ``PATH_INFO`` can be derived by stripping ``root_path`` from ``path``\n* ``QUERY_STRING`` is ``query_string``\n* ``CONTENT_TYPE`` can be extracted from ``headers``\n* ``CONTENT_LENGTH`` can be extracted from ``headers``\n* ``SERVER_NAME`` and ``SERVER_PORT`` are in ``server``\n* ``REMOTE_HOST``/``REMOTE_ADDR`` and ``REMOTE_PORT`` are in ``client``\n* ``SERVER_PROTOCOL`` is encoded in ``http_version``\n* ``wsgi.url_scheme`` is ``scheme``\n* ``wsgi.input`` is a ``StringIO`` based around the ``http.request`` messages\n* ``wsgi.errors`` is directed by the wrapper as needed\n\nThe ``start_response`` callable maps similarly to ``http.response.start``:\n\n* The ``status`` argument becomes ``status``, with the reason phrase dropped.\n* ``response_headers`` maps to ``headers``\n\nYielding content from the WSGI application maps to sending\n``http.response.body`` messages.\n\n\nWSGI encoding differences\n-------------------------\n\nThe WSGI specification (as defined in PEP 3333) specifies that all strings\nsent to or from the server must be of the ``str`` type but only contain\ncodepoints in the ISO-8859-1 (\"latin-1\") range. This was due to it originally\nbeing designed for Python 2 and its different set of string types.\n\nThe ASGI HTTP and WebSocket specifications instead specify each entry of the\n``scope`` dict as either a byte string or a Unicode string. HTTP, being an\nolder protocol, is sometimes imperfect at specifying encoding, so some\ndecisions of what is Unicode versus bytes may not be obvious.\n\n* ``path``: URLs can have both percent-encoded and UTF-8 encoded sections.\n  Because decoding these is often done by the underlying server (or sometimes\n  even proxies in the path), this is a Unicode string, fully decoded from both\n  UTF-8 encoding and percent encodings.\n\n* ``headers``: These are byte strings of the exact byte sequences sent by the\n  client/to be sent by the server. While modern HTTP standards say that headers\n  should be ASCII, older ones did not and allowed a wider range of characters.\n  Frameworks/applications should decode headers as they deem appropriate.\n\n* ``query_string``: Unlike the ``path``, this is not as subject to server\n  interference and so is presented as its raw byte string version,\n  percent-encoded.\n\n* ``root_path``: Unicode string to match ``path``.\n\n\nVersion History\n---------------\n\n\n* 2.0 (2017-11-28): Initial non-channel-layer based ASGI spec\n\n\nCopyright\n---------\n\n\nThis document has been placed A schedule optimizer determines how to group active threads from the same warp together into SIMT units. This retains the high throughput of SIMT execution as in prior NVIDIA GPUs, but with much more flexibility: threads can now diverge and reconverge at sub-warp granularity.\nIndependent Thread Scheduling can lead to a rather different set of threads participating in the executed code than intended if the developer made assumptions about warp-synchronicity\n2\nof previous hardware architectures. In particular, any warp-synchronous code (such as synchronization-free, intra-warp reductions) should be revisited to ensure compatibility with NVIDIA Volta and beyond. See\nCompute Capability 7.x\nfor further details.\nNote\nThe threads of a warp that are participating in the current instruction are called the\nactive\nthreads, whereas threads not on the current instruction are\ninactive\n(disabled). Threads can be inactive for a variety of reasons including having exited earlier than other threads of their warp, having taken a different branch path than the branch path currently executed by the warp, or being the last threads of a block whose number of threads is not a multiple of the warp size.\nIf a non-atomic instruction executed by a warp writes to the same location in global or shared memory for more than one of the threads of the warp, the number of serialized writes that occur to that location varies depending on the compute capability of the device (see\nCompute Capability 5.x\n,\nCompute Capability 6.x\n, and\nCompute Capability 7.x\n), and which thread performs the final write is undefined.\nIf an\natomic\ninstruction executed by a warp reads, modifies, and writes to the same location in global memory for more than one of the threads of the warp, each read/modify/write to that location occurs and they are all serialized, but the order in which they occur is undefined.\n4.2.\nHardware Multithreading\n\nThe execution context (program counters, registers, and so on) for each warp processed by a multiprocessor is maintained on-chip during the entire lifetime of the warp. Therefore, switching from one execution context to another has no cost, and at every instruction issue time, a warp scheduler selects a warp that has threads ready to execute its next instruction (the\nactive threads\nof the warp) and issues the instruction to those threads.\nIn particular, each multiprocessor has a set of 32-bit registers that are partitioned among the warps, and a\nparallel data cache\nor\nshared memory\nthat is partitioned among the thread blocks.\nThe number of blocks and warps that can reside and be processed together on the multiprocessor for a given kernel depends on the amount of registers and shared memory used by the kernel and the amount of registers and shared memory available on the multiprocessor. There are also a maximum number of resident blocks and a maximum number of resident warps per multiprocessor. These limits as well the amount of registers and shared memory available on the multiprocessor are a function of the compute capability of the device and are given in\nCompute Capabilities\n. If there are not enough registers or shared memory available per multiprocessor to process at least one block, the kernel will fail to launch.\nThe total number of warps in a block is as follows:\n\\(\\text{ceil}\\left( \\frac{T}{W_{size}},1 \\right)\\)\nT\nis the number of threads per block,\nWsize\nis the warp size, which is equal to 32,\nceil(x, y) is equal to x rounded up to the nearest multiple of y.\nThe total number of registers and total amount of shared memory allocated for a block are documented in the CUDA Occupancy Calculator provided in the CUDA Toolkit.\n2\nThe term\nwarp-synchronous\nrefers to code that implicitly assumes threads in the same warp are synchronized at every instruction.\n5.\nPerformance Guidelines\n\n5.1.\nOverall Performance Optimization Strategies\n\nPerformance optimization revolves around four basic strategies:\nMaximize parallel execution to achieve maximum utilization;\nOptimize memory usage to achieve maximum memory throughput;\nOptimize instruction usage to achieve maximum instruction throughput;\nMinimize memory thrashing.\nWhich strategies will yield the best performance gain for a particular portion of an application depends on the performance limiters for that portion; optimizing instruction usage of a kernel that is mostly limited by memory accesses will not yield any significant performance gain, for example. Optimization efforts should therefore be constantly directed by measuring and monitoring the performance limiters, for example using the CUDA profiler. Also, comparing the floating-point operation throughput or memory throughput—whichever makes more sense—of a particular kernel to the corresponding peak theoretical throughput of the device indicates how much room for improvement there is for the kernel.\n5.2.\nMaximize Utilization\n\nTo maximize utilization the application should be structured in a way that it exposes as much parallelism as possible and efficiently maps this parallelism to the various components of the system to keep them busy most of the time.\n5.2.1.\nApplication Level\n\nAt a high level, the application should maximize parallel execution between the host, the devices, and the bus connecting the host to the devices, by using asynchronous functions calls and streams as described in\nAsynchronous Concurrent Execution\n. It should assign to each processor the type of work it does best: serial workloads to the host; parallel workloads to the devices.\nFor the parallel workloads, at points in the algorithm where parallelism is broken because some threads need to synchronize in order to share data with each other, there are two cases: Either these threads belong to the same block, in which case they should use\n__syncthreads()\nand share data through shared memory within the same kernel invocation, or they belong to different blocks, in which case they must share data through global memory using two separate kernel invocations, one for writing to and one for reading from global memory. The second case is much less optimal since it adds the overhead of extra kernel invocations and global memory traffic. Its occurrence should therefore be minimized by mapping the algorithm to the CUDA programming model in such a way that the computations that require inter-thread communication are performed within a single thread block as much as possible.\n5.2.2.\nDevice Level\n\nAt a lower level, the application should maximize parallel execution between the multiprocessors of a device.\nMultiple kernels can execute concurrently on a device, so maximum utilization can also be achieved by using streams to enable enough kernels to execute concurrently as described in\nAsynchronous Concurrent Execution\n.\n5.2.3.\nMultiprocessor Level\n\nAt an even lower level, the application should maximize parallel execution between the various functional units within a multiprocessor.\nAs described in\nHardware Multithreading\n, a GPU multiprocessor primarily relies on thread-level parallelism to maximize utilization of its functional units. Utilization is therefore directly linked to the number of resident warps. At every instruction issue time, a warp scheduler selects an instruction that is ready to execute. This instruction can be another independent instruction of the same warp, exploiting instruction-level parallelism, or more commonly an instruction of another warp, exploiting thread-level parallelism. If a ready to execute instruction is selected it is issued to the\nactive\nthreads of the warp. The number of clock cycles it takes for a warp to be ready to execute its next instruction is called the\nlatency\n, and full utilization is achieved when all warp schedulers always have some instruction to issue for some warp at e\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 328000:332000]\nte capability 6.x\n#elif __CUDA_ARCH__ >= 500\n// Device code path for compute capability 5.x\n#elif !defined(__CUDA_ARCH__)\n// Host code path\n#endif\n}\n7.1.4.\nUndefined behavior\n\nA ‘cross-execution space’ call has undefined behavior when:\n__CUDA_ARCH__\nis defined, a call from within a\n__global__\n,\n__device__\nor\n__host__\n__device__\nfunction to a\n__host__\nfunction.\n__CUDA_ARCH__\nis undefined, a call from within a\n__host__\nfunction to a\n__device__\nfunction.\n9\n7.1.5.\n__noinline__ and __forceinline__\n\nThe compiler inlines any\n__device__\nfunction when deemed appropriate.\nThe\n__noinline__\nfunction qualifier can be used as a hint for the compiler not to inline the function if possible.\nThe\n__forceinline__\nfunction qualifier can be used to force the compiler to inline the function.\nThe\n__noinline__\nand\n__forceinline__\nfunction qualifiers cannot be used together, and neither function qualifier can be applied to an inline function.\n7.1.6.\n__inline_hint__\n\nThe\n__inline_hint__\nqualifier enables more aggressive inlining in the compiler. Unlike\n__forceinline__\n, it does not imply that the function is inline. It can be used to improve inlining across modules when using LTO.\nNeither the\n__noinline__\nnor the\n__forceinline__\nfunction qualifier can be used with the\n__inline_hint__\nfunction qualifier.\n7.2.\nVariable Memory Space Specifiers\n\nVariable memory space specifiers denote the memory location on the device of a variable.\nAn automatic variable declared in device code without any of the\n__device__\n,\n__shared__\nand\n__constant__\nmemory space specifiers described in this section generally resides in a register. However in some cases the compiler might choose to place it in local memory, which can have adverse performance consequences as detailed in\nDevice Memory Accesses\n.\n7.2.1.\n__device__\n\nThe\n__device__\nmemory space specifier declares a variable that resides on the device.\nAt most one of the other memory space specifiers defined in the next three sections may be used together with\n__device__\nto further denote which memory space the variable belongs to. If none of them is present, the variable:\nResides in global memory space,\nHas the lifetime of the CUDA context in which it is created,\nHas a distinct object per device,\nIs accessible from all the threads within the grid and from the host through the runtime library\n(cudaGetSymbolAddress()\n/\ncudaGetSymbolSize()\n/\ncudaMemcpyToSymbol()\n/\ncudaMemcpyFromSymbol()\n).\n7.2.2.\n__constant__\n\nThe\n__constant__\nmemory space specifier, optionally used together with\n__device__\n, declares a variable that:\nResides in constant memory space,\nHas the lifetime of the CUDA context in which it is created,\nHas a distinct object per device,\nIs accessible from all the threads within the grid and from the host through the runtime library (\ncudaGetSymbolAddress()\n/\ncudaGetSymbolSize()\n/\ncudaMemcpyToSymbol()\n/\ncudaMemcpyFromSymbol()\n).\nThe behavior of modifying a constant from the host while there is a concurrent grid that access that constant at any point of this grid’s lifetime is undefined.\n7.2.3.\n__shared__\n\nThe\n__shared__\nmemory space specifier, optionally used together with\n__device__\n, declares a variable that:\nResides in the shared memory space of a thread block,\nHas the lifetime of the block,\nHas a distinct object per block,\nIs only accessible from all the threads within the block,\nDoes not have a constant address.\nWhen declaring a variable in shared memory as an external array such as\nextern\n__shared__\nfloat\nshared\n[];\nthe size of the array is determined at launch time (see\nExecution Configuration\n). All variables declared in this fashion, start at the same address in memory, so that the layout of the variables in the array must be explicitly managed through offsets. For example, if one wants the equivalent of\nshort\narray0\n[\n128\n];\nfloat\narray1\n[\n64\n];\nint\narray2\n[\n256\n];\nin dynamically allocated shared memory, one could declare and initialize the arrays the following way:\nextern\n__shared__\nfloat\narray\n[];\n__de\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 676000:680000]\nle, performance and other properties of a program which uses the device runtime.\n9.4.3.1.\nRuntime\n\n9.4.3.1.1.\nMemory Footprint\n\nThe device runtime system software reserves memory for various management purposes, in particular a reservation for tracking pending grid launches. Configuration controls are available to reduce the size of this reservation in exchange for certain launch limitations. See\nConfiguration Options\n, below, for details.\n9.4.3.1.2.\nPending Kernel Launches\n\nWhen a kernel is launched, all associated configuration and parameter data is tracked until the kernel completes. This data is stored within a system-managed launch pool.\nThe size of the fixed-size launch pool is configurable by calling\ncudaDeviceSetLimit()\nfrom the host and specifying\ncudaLimitDevRuntimePendingLaunchCount\n.\n9.4.3.1.3.\nConfiguration Options\n\nResource allocation for the device runtime system software is controlled via the\ncudaDeviceSetLimit()\nAPI from the host program. Limits must be set before any kernel is launched, and may not be changed while the GPU is actively running programs.\nThe following named limits may be set:\nLimit\nBehavior\ncudaLimitDevRuntimePendingLaunchCount\nControls the amount of memory set aside for buffering kernel launches and events which have not yet begun to execute, due either to unresolved dependencies or lack of execution resources. When the buffer is full, an attempt to allocate a launch slot during a device side kernel launch will fail and return\ncudaErrorLaunchOutOfResources\n, while an attempt to allocate an event slot will fail and return\ncudaErrorMemoryAllocation\n. The default number of launch slots is 2048. Applications may increase the number of launch and/or event slots by setting\ncudaLimitDevRuntimePendingLaunchCount\n. The number of event slots allocated is twice the value of that limit.\ncudaLimitStackSize\nControls the stack size in bytes of each GPU thread. The CUDA driver automatically increases the per-thread stack size for each kernel launch as needed. This size isn’t reset back to the original value after each launch. To set the per-thread stack size to a different value,\ncudaDeviceSetLimit()\ncan be called to set this limit. The stack will be immediately resized, and if necessary, the device will block until all preceding requested tasks are complete.\ncudaDeviceGetLimit()\ncan be called to get the current per-thread stack size.\n9.4.3.1.4.\nMemory Allocation and Lifetime\n\ncudaMalloc()\nand\ncudaFree()\nhave distinct semantics between the host and device environments. When invoked from the host,\ncudaMalloc()\nallocates a new region from unused device memory. When invoked from the device runtime these functions map to device-side\nmalloc()\nand\nfree()\n. This implies that within the device environment the total allocatable memory is limited to the device\nmalloc()\nheap size, which may be smaller than the available unused device memory. Also, it is an error to invoke\ncudaFree()\nfrom the host program on a pointer which was allocated by\ncudaMalloc()\non the device or vice-versa.\ncudaMalloc()\non Host\ncudaMalloc()\non Device\ncudaFree()\non Host\nSupported\nNot Supported\ncudaFree()\non Device\nNot Supported\nSupported\nAllocation limit\nFree device memory\ncudaLimitMallocHeapSize\n9.4.3.1.5.\nSM Id and Warp Id\n\nNote that in PTX\n%smid\nand\n%warpid\nare defined as volatile values. The device runtime may reschedule thread blocks onto different SMs in order to more efficiently manage resources. As such, it is unsafe to rely upon\n%smid\nor\n%warpid\nremaining unchanged across the lifetime of a thread or thread block.\n9.4.3.1.6.\nECC Errors\n\nNo notification of ECC errors is available to code within a CUDA kernel. ECC errors are reported at the host side once the entire launch tree has completed. Any ECC errors which arise during execution of a nested program will either generate an exception or continue execution (depending upon error and configuration).\n9.5.\nCDP2 vs CDP1\n\nThis section summarises the differences between, and the compati\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 688000:692000]\nCDP2 version of document.\nThe ordering of kernel launches from the device runtime follows CUDA Stream ordering semantics. Within a thread block, all kernel launches into the same stream are executed in-order. With multiple threads in the same thread block launching into the same stream, the ordering within the stream is dependent on the thread scheduling within the block, which may be controlled with synchronization primitives such as\n__syncthreads()\n.\nNote that because streams are shared by all threads within a thread block, the implicit\nNULL\nstream is also shared. If multiple threads in a thread block launch into the implicit stream, then these launches will be executed in-order. If concurrency is desired, explicit named streams should be used.\nDynamic Parallelism\nenables concurrency to be expressed more easily within a program; however, the device runtime introduces no new concurrency guarantees within the CUDA execution model. There is no guarantee of concurrent execution between any number of different thread blocks on a device.\nThe lack of concurrency guarantee extends to parent thread blocks and their child grids. When a parent thread block launches a child grid, the child is not guaranteed to begin execution until the parent thread block reaches an explicit synchronization point (such as\ncudaDeviceSynchronize()\n).\nWarning\nExplicit synchronization with child kernels from a parent block (i.e. using\ncudaDeviceSynchronize()\nin device code) is deprecated in CUDA 11.6, removed for compute_90+ compilation, and is slated for full removal in a future CUDA release.\nWhile concurrency will often easily be achieved, it may vary as a function of deviceconfiguration, application workload, and runtime scheduling. It is therefore unsafe to depend upon any concurrency between different thread blocks.\n9.6.1.1.6.\nDevice Management (CDP1)\n\nSee\nDevice Management\n, above, for CDP2 version of document.\nThere is no multi-GPU support from the device runtime; the device runtime is only capable of operating on the device upon which it is currently executing. It is permitted, however, to query properties for any CUDA capable device in the system.\n9.6.1.2.\nMemory Model (CDP1)\n\nSee\nMemory Model\n, above, for CDP2 version of document.\nParent and child grids share the same global and constant memory storage, but have distinct local and shared memory.\n9.6.1.2.1.\nCoherence and Consistency (CDP1)\n\nSee\nCoherence and Consistency\n, above, for CDP2 version of document.\n9.6.1.2.1.1.\nGlobal Memory (CDP1)\n\nSee\nGlobal Memory\n, above, for CDP2 version of document.\nParent and child grids have coherent access to global memory, with weak consistency guarantees between child and parent. There are two points in the execution of a child grid when its view of memory is fully consistent with the parent thread: when the child grid is invoked by the parent, and when the child grid completes as signaled by a synchronization API invocation in the parent thread.\nWarning\nExplicit synchronization with child kernels from a parent block (i.e. using\ncudaDeviceSynchronize()\nin device code) is deprecated in CUDA 11.6, removed for compute_90+ compilation, and is slated for full removal in a future CUDA release.\nAll global memory operations in the parent thread prior to the child grid’s invocation are visible to the child grid. All memory operations of the child grid are visible to the parent after the parent has synchronized on the child grid’s completion.\nIn the following example, the child grid executing\nchild_launch\nis only guaranteed to see the modifications to\ndata\nmade before the child grid was launched. Since thread 0 of the parent is performing the launch, the child will be consistent with the memory seen by thread 0 of the parent. Due to the first\n__syncthreads()\ncall, the child will see\ndata[0]=0\n,\ndata[1]=1\n, …,\ndata[255]=255\n(without the\n__syncthreads()\ncall, only\ndata[0]\nwould be guaranteed to be seen by the child). When the child grid returns, thread 0 is guaranteed \n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 728000:732000]\nnches when the fixed-size pool is full.\nThe size of the fixed-size launch pool is configurable by calling\ncudaDeviceSetLimit()\nfrom the host and specifying\ncudaLimitDevRuntimePendingLaunchCount\n.\n9.6.3.3.1.4.\nConfiguration Options (CDP1)\n\nSee\nConfiguration Options\n, above, for CDP2 version of document.\nResource allocation for the device runtime system software is controlled via the\ncudaDeviceSetLimit()\nAPI from the host program. Launches may be performed deeper than this, but explicit synchronization deeper than this limit will return the\ncudaErrorLaunchMaxDepthExceeded\n. However, memsets of the allocations can be stream captured.\n11.13.4.\nPointer Attributes\n\nThe\ncuPointerGetAttributes\nquery works on stream ordered allocations. Since stream ordered allocations are not context associated, querying\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_CONTEXT\nwill succeed but return NULL in\n*data\n. The attribute\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_DEVICE_ORDINAL\ncan be used to determine the location of the allocation: this can be useful when selecting a context for making p2h2p copies using\ncudaMemcpyPeerAsync\n. The attribute\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_MEMPOOL_HANDLE\nwas added in CUDA 11.3 and can be useful for debugging and for confirming which pool an allocation comes from before doing IPC.\n12.\nGraph Memory Nodes\n\n12.1.\nIntroduction\n\nGraph memory nodes allow graphs to create and own memory allocations. Graph memory nodes have GPU ordered lifetime semantics, which dictate when memory is allowed to be accessed on the device. These GPU ordered lifetime semantics enable driver-managed memory reuse, and match those of the stream ordered allocation APIs\ncudaMallocAsync\nand\ncudaFreeAsync\n, which may be captured when creating a graph.\nGraph allocations have fixed addresses over the life of a graph including repeated instantiations and launches. This allows the memory to be directly referenced by other operations within the graph without the need of a graph update, even when CUDA changes the backing physical memory. Within a graph, allocations whose graph ordered lifetimes do not overlap may use the same underlying physical memory.\nCUDA may reuse the same physical memory for allocations across multiple graphs, aliasing virtual address mappings according to the GPU ordered lifetime semantics. For example when different graphs are launched into the same stream, CUDA may virtually alias the same physical memory to satisfy the needs of allocations which have single-graph lifetimes.\n12.2.\nSupport and Compatibility\n\nGraph memory nodes require an 11.4 capable CUDA driver and support for the stream ordered allocator on the GPU. The following snippet shows how to check for support on a given device.\nint\ndriverVersion\n=\n0\n;\nint\ndeviceSupportsMemoryPools\n=\n0\n;\nint\ndeviceSupportsMemoryNodes\n=\n0\n;\ncudaDriverGetVersion\n(\n&\ndriverVersion\n);\nif\n(\ndriverVersion\n>=\n11020\n)\n{\n// avoid invalid value error in cudaDeviceGetAttribute\ncudaDeviceGetAttribute\n(\n&\ndeviceSupportsMemoryPools\n,\ncudaDevAttrMemoryPoolsSupported\n,\ndevice\n);\n}\ndeviceSupportsMemoryNodes\n=\n(\ndriverVersion\n>=\n11040\n)\n&&\n(\ndeviceSupportsMemoryPools\n!=\n0\n);\nDoing the attribute query inside the driver version check avoids an invalid value return code on 11.0 and 11.1 drivers. Be aware that the compute sanitizer emits warnings when it detects CUDA returning error codes, and a version check before reading the attribute will avoid this. Graph memory nodes are only supported on driver versions 11.4 and newer.\n12.3.\nAPI Fundamentals\n\nGraph memory nodes are graph nodes representing either memory allocation or free actions. As a shorthand, nodes that allocate memory are called allocation nodes. Likewise, nodes that free memory are called free nodes. Allocations created by allocation nodes are called graph allocations. CUDA assigns virtual addresses for the graph allocation at node creation time. While these virtual addresses are fixed for the lifetime of the allocation node, the allocation contents are not persistent past the freeing operation and may be overwritten by accesses referring to a different allocation.\nGraph allocations are considered recreated every time a graph runs. A graph allocation’s lifetime, which differs from the node’s lifetime, begins when GPU execution reaches the allocating graph node and ends when one of the following occurs:\nGPU execution reaches the freeing graph node\nGPU execution reaches the freeing\ncudaFreeAsync()\nstream call\nimmediately upon the freeing call to\ncudaFree()\nNote\nGraph destruction does not automatically free any live graph-allocated memory, even though it ends the lifetime of the allocation node. The allocation must subsequently be freed in another graph, or using\ncudaFreeAsync()\n/cudaFree()\n.\nJust like other\nGraph Structure\n, graph memory nodes are ordered within a graph by dependency edges. A program must guarantee that operations accessing graph memory:\nare ordered after the allocation node\nare ordered before the operation freeing the memory\nGraph allocation lifetimes begin and usually end according to GPU execution (as opposed to API invocation). GPU ordering is the order that work runs on the GPU as opposed to the order that the work is enqueued or described. Thus, graph allocations are considered ‘GPU ordered.’\n12.3.1.\nGraph Node APIs\n\nGraph memory nodes may be explicitly created with the memory node creation APIs,\ncudaGraphAddMemAllocNode\nand\ncudaGraphAddMemFreeNode\n. The address allocated by\ncudaGraphAddMemAllocNode\nis returned to the user in the\ndptr\nfield of the passed\nCUDA_MEM_ALLOC_NODE_PARAMS\nstructure. All operations using graph allocations inside the allocating graph must be ordered after the allocating node. Similarly, any free nodes must be ordered after all uses of the allocation within the graph.\ncudaGraphAddMemFreeNode\ncreates free nodes.\nIn the following figure, there is an example graph with an alloc and a free node. Kernel nodes\na\n,\nb\n, and\nc\nare ordered after the allocation node and before the free node such that the kernels can access the allocation. Kernel node\ne\nis not ordered after the alloc node and therefore cannot safely access the memory. Kernel node\nd\nis not ordered before the free node, therefore it cannot safely access the memory.\nFigure 31\nKernel Nodes\n\nThe following code snippet establishes the graph in this figure:\n// Create the graph - it starts out empty\n\ncudaGraphCreate(&graph, 0);\n\n\n\n// parameters for a basic allocation\n\ncudaMemAllocNodeParams params = {};\n\nparams.poolProps.allocType = cudaMemAllocationTypePinned;\n\nparams.poolProps.location.type = cudaMemLocationTypeDevice;\n\n// specify device 0 as the resident device\n\nparams.poolProps.location.id = 0;\n\nparams.bytesize = size;\n\n\n\ncudaGraphAddMemAllocNode(&allocNode, graph, NULL, 0, &params);\n\nnodeParams->kernelParams[0] = params.dptr;\n\ncudaGraphAddKernelNode(&a, graph, &allocNode, 1, &nodeParams);\n\ncudaGraphAddKernelNode(&b, graph, &a, 1, &nodeParams);\n\ncudaGraphAddKernelNode(&c, graph, &a, 1, &nodeParams);\n\ncudaGraphNode_t dependencies[2];\n\n// kernel nodes b and c are using the graph allocation, so the freeing node must depend on them.  Since the dependency of node b on node a establishes an indirect dependency, the free node does not need to explicitly depend on node a.\n\ndependencies[0] = b;\n\ndependencies[1] = c;\n\ncudaGraphAddMemFreeNode(&freeNode, graph, dependencies, 2, params.dptr);\n\n// free node does not depend on kernel node d, so it must not access the freed graph allocation.\n\ncudaGraphAddKernelNode(&d, graph, &c, 1, &nodeParams);\n\n\n\n// node e does not depend on the allocation node, so it must not access the allocation.  This would be true even if the freeNode depended on kernel node e.\n\ncudaGraphAddKernelNode(&e, graph, NULL, 0, &nodeParams);\n12.3.2\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 800000:804000]\nm which would otherwise need to free all the allocations owned by a graph before each relaunch.\nNote\nThe\ncudaGraphInstantiateFlagAutoFreeOnLaunch\nflag does not change the behavior of graph destruction. Memory Reuse\n\nCUDA reuses memory in two ways:\nVirtual and physical memory reuse within a graph is based on virtual address assignment, like in the stream ordered allocator.\nPhysical memory reuse between graphs is done with virtual aliasing: different graphs can map the same physical memory to their unique virtual addresses.\n12.4.1.\nAddress Reuse within a Graph\n\nCUDA may reuse memory within a graph by assigning the same virtual address ranges to different allocations whose lifetimes do not overlap. Since virtual addresses may be reused, pointers to different allocations with disjoint lifetimes are not guaranteed to be unique.\nThe following figure shows adding a new allocation node (2) that can reuse the address freed by a dependent node (1).\nFigure 32\nAdding New Alloc Node 2\n\nThe following figure shows adding a new alloc node (4). The new alloc node is not dependent on the free node (2) so cannot reuse the address from the associated alloc node (2). If the alloc node (2) used the address freed by free node (1), the new alloc node 3 would need a new address.\nFigure 33\nAdding New Alloc Node 3\n\n12.4.2.\nPhysical Memory Management and Sharing\n\nCUDA is responsible for mapping physical memory to the virtual address before the allocating node is reached in GPU order. As an optimization for memory footprint and mapping overhead, multiple graphs may use the same physical memory for distinct allocations if they will not run simultaneously; however, physical pages cannot be reused if they are bound to more than one executing graph at the same time, or to a graph allocation which remains unfreed.\nCUDA may update physical memory mappings at any time during graph instantiation, launch, or execution. CUDA may also introduce synchronization between future graph launches in order to prevent live graph \n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 948000:955646]\nre cache and L2 or in L2 only) can be partially configured on a per-access basis using modifiers to the load instruction.\n16.5.2.\nGlobal Memory\n\nGlobal memory behaves the same way as in devices of compute capability 5.x (See\nGlobal Memory\n).\n16.5.3.\nShared Memory\n\nShared memory behaves the same way as in devices of compute capability 5.x (See\nShared Memory\n).\n16.6.\nCompute Capability 7.x\n\n16.6.1.\nArchitecture\n\nAn SM consists of:\n64 FP32 cores for single-precision arithmetic operations,\n32 FP64 cores for double-precision arithmetic operations,\n34\n64 INT32 cores for integer math,\n8 mixed-precision Tensor Cores for deep learning matrix arithmetic\n16 special function units for single-precision floating-point transcendental functions,\n4 warp schedulers.\nAn SM statically distributes its warps among its schedulers. Then, at every instruction issue time, each scheduler issues one instruction for one of its assigned warps that is ready to execute, if any.\nAn SM has:\na read-only constant cache that is shared by all functional units and speeds up reads from the constant memory space, which resides in device memory,\na unified data cache and shared memory with a total size of 128 KB (\nVolta\n) or 96 KB (\nTuring\n).\nShared memory is partitioned out of unified data cache, and can be configured to various sizes (See\nShared Memory\n.) The remaining data cache serves as an L1 cache and is also used by the texture unit that implements the various addressing and data filtering modes mentioned in\nTexture and Surface Memory\n.\n16.6.2.\nIndependent Thread Scheduling\n\nThe\nVolta\narchitecture introduces\nIndependent Thread Scheduling\namong threads in a warp, enabling intra-warp synchronization patterns previously unavailable and simplifying code changes when porting CPU code. However, this can lead to a rather different set of threads participating in the executed code than intended if the developer made assumptions about warp-synchronicity of previous hardware architectures.\nBelow are code patterns of concern and suggested corrective actions for Volta-safe code.\nFor applications using warp intrinsics (\n__shfl*\n,\n__any\n,\n__all\n,\n__ballot\n), it is necessary that developers port their code to the new, safe, synchronizing counterpart, with the\n*_sync\nsuffix. The new warp intrinsics take in a mask of threads that explicitly define which lanes (threads of a warp) must participate in the warp intrinsic. See\nWarp Vote Functions\nand\nWarp Shuffle Functions\nfor details.\nSince the intrinsics are available with CUDA 9.0+, (if necessary) code can be executed conditionally with the following preprocessor macro:\n#if defined(CUDART_VERSION) && CUDART_VERSION >= 9000\n// *_sync intrinsic\n#endif\nThese intrinsics are available on all architectures, not just\nVolta\nor\nTuring\n, and in most cases a single code-base will suffice for all architectures. Note, however, that for\nPascal\nand earlier architectures, all threads in mask must execute the same warp intrinsic instruction in convergence, and the union of all values in mask must be equal to the warp’s active mask. The following code pattern is valid on\nVolta\n, but not on\nPascal\nor earlier architectures.\nif\n(\ntid\n%\nwarpSize\n<\n16\n)\n{\n...\nfloat\nswapped\n=\n__shfl_xor_sync\n(\n0xffffffff\n,\nval\n,\n16\n);\n...\n}\nelse\n{\n...\nfloat\nswapped\n=\n__shfl_xor_sync\n(\n0xffffffff\n,\nval\n,\n16\n);\n...\n}\nThe replacement for\n__ballot(1)\nis\n__activemask()\n. Note that threads within a warp can diverge even within a single code path. As a result,\n__activemask()\nand\n__ballot(1)\nmay return only a subset of the threads on the current code path. The following invalid code example sets bit\ni\nof\noutput\nto 1 when\ndata[i]\nis greater than\nthreshold\n.\n__activemask()\nis used in an attempt to enable cases where\ndataLen\nis not a multiple of 32.\n// Sets bit in output[] to 1 if the correspond element in data[i]\n// is greater than 'threshold', using 32 threads in a warp.\nfor\n(\nint\ni\n=\nwarpLane\n;\ni\n<\ndataLen\n;\ni\n+=\nwarpSize\n)\n{\nunsigned\nactive\n=\n__activemask\n();\nunsigned\nbitPack\n=\n__ballot_sync\n(\nactive\n,\ndata\n[\ni\n]\n>\nthreshold\n);\nif\n(\nwarpLane\n==\n0\n)\n{\noutput\n[\ni\n/\n32\n]\n=\nbitPack\n;\n}\n}\nThis code is invalid because CUDA does not guarantee that the warp will diverge ONLY at the loop condition. Assumptions that code is executed in lockstep or that reads/writes from separate threads are visible across a warp without synchronization are invalid.\n__shared__\nfloat\ns_buff\n[\nBLOCK_SIZE\n];\ns_buff\n[\ntid\n]\n=\nval\n;\n__syncthreads\n();\n// Inter-warp reduction\nfor\n(\nint\ni\n=\nBLOCK_SIZE\n/\n2\n;\ni\n>=\n32\n;\ni\n/=\n2\n)\n{\nif\n(\ntid\n<\ni\n)\n{\ns_buff\n[\ntid\n]\n+=\ns_buff\n[\ntid\n+\ni\n];\n}\n__syncthreads\n();\n}\n// Intra-warp reduction\n// Butterfly reduction simplifies syncwarp mask\nif\n(\ntid\n<\n32\n)\n{\nfloat\ntemp\n;\ntemp\n=\ns_buff\n[\ntid\n^\n16\n];\n__syncwarp\n();\ns_buff\n[\ntid\n]\n+=\ntemp\n;\n__syncwarp\n();\ntemp\n=\ns_buff\n[\ntid\n^\n8\n];\n__syncwarp\n();\ns_buff\n[\ntid\n]\n+=\ntemp\n;\n__syncwarp\n();\ntemp\n=\ns_buff\n[\ntid\n^\n4\n];\n__syncwarp\n();\ns_buff\n[\ntid\n]\n+=\ntemp\n;\n__syncwarp\n();\ntemp\n=\ns_buff\n[\ntid\n^\n2\n];\n__syncwarp\n();\ns_buff\n[\ntid\n]\n+=\ntemp\n;\n__syncwarp\n();\n}\nif\n(\ntid\n==\n0\n)\n{\n*\noutput\n=\ns_buff\n[\n0\n]\n+\ns_buff\n[\n1\n];\n}\n__syncthreads\n();\nAlthough\n__syncthreads()\nhas been consistently documented as synchronizing all threads in the thread block,\nPascal\nand prior architectures could only enforce synchronization at the warp level. In certain cases, this allowed a barrier to succeed without being executed by every thread as long as at least some thread in every warp reached the barrier.
